JSS 1 MATHEMATICS SCHEME OF WORK
FIRST TERM
Week Topic                          Content

1     Whole Numbers                 Place value, ordering, and properties of whole numbers.



2     LCM (Least Common Multiple)   Finding LCM using multiples and prime factorization.



3     HCF (Highest Common Factor)   Finding HCF using factorization methods.



4     Counting in Base 2            Introduction to the binary number system.

5     Midterm Exams                 –

6     Conversion of Numbers         Conversion of Base 10 numerals to binary numbers (step-
                                    by-step).


7     Midterm Break                 –

8     Fractions I                   Types of fractions (proper, improper, mixed),
                                    simplification.


9     Fractions II                  Equivalent fractions, comparison of fractions.

10    Basic Operations on Whole     Addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division.
      Numbers


11–   Revision & Exams              –
13




                                         10
          GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE

JSS 1 MATHEMATICS SCHEME OF WORK
SECOND TERM
Week Topic                                  Content

1     Addition & Subtraction of Fractions   With the same and different denominators.



2     Multiplication & Division of          Solving real-life problems with fractions.
      Fractions


3     Estimation & Approximation            Rounding off numbers, significant figures.

4     Numbers in Base 2 – Addition          Binary addition rules.

5     Midterm Exams                         –

6     Numbers in Base 2 – Subtraction       Binary subtraction rules.

7     Midterm Break                         –

8     Numbers in Base 2 – Multiplication    Binary multiplication rules.



9     Algebraic Processes I                 Use of symbols, coefficients, and algebraic
                                            expressions.


10    Simplification of Algebraic           Expanding brackets, collecting like terms.
      Expressions


11–13 Revision & Exams                      –




                                            11
          GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE

JSS 1 MATHEMATICS SCHEME OF WORK
THIRD TERM
Week Topic                      Content

1     Simple Equations          Solving one-variable linear equations.

2     Plane Shapes              Properties of triangles, quadrilaterals, and circles.

3     Three-Dimensional         Cubes, cuboids, cylinders, spheres, and cones.
      Figures



4     Constructions             Basic geometric constructions using ruler and compass.




5     Midterm Exams             –

6     Angles                    Types of angles, angle sum properties of triangles &
                                quadrilaterals.



7     Midterm Break             –

8     Everyday Statistics I     Need for statistics, importance in daily life.

9     Everyday Statistics II    Methods of data collection.

10    Everyday Statistics III   Data presentation: tables, bar charts, pie charts.

11–13 Revision & Exams          –

JSS1 PHYSICAL AND HEALTH EDUCATION SCHEME OF WORK
FIRST TERM
Week Topic                                  Content

1     Meaning of Physical Fitness           Definition of physical fitness; difference between
                                            fitness and health


2     Components of Physical Fitness        Strength, endurance, flexibility, agility, speed,
                                            balance, coordination


3     Characteristics of a Physically Fit   Features such as stamina, resistance to illness,
      Person                                alertness, efficiency


4     Importance of Physical Fitness        Benefits: health promotion, body development,
                                            productivity, prevention of diseases




5     Midterm Exams                         —

6     Exercises to Develop Strength,        Examples: push-ups, sit-ups, squats, jogging,
      Endurance and Flexibility             stretching, rope skipping


7     Midterm Break                         —

8     Safety Precautions while Performing   Warm-up, cool-down, hydration, use of proper kits,
      Exercises                             avoiding overstrain


9     Meaning of Recreation, Leisure and    Definitions with examples of recreational and leisure
      Dance                                 activities


10    Differences between Dance, Leisure    Distinct features and examples of each
      and Recreation


11    Revision                              —

12    Examination                           —

13    Closing                               —

Week Topic                                      Content

      Techniques                                performance

      and Techniques)

7     Midterm Break                             —

      Throw

      Events                                    facilities

                                                team play, safety measures

FIRST TERM


Week Topic                       Content

1     God and His Creation       Who is God? (Gen. 1:1; Jer. 10:12)

2     The Creation Story         Biblical account of creation in Genesis 1

3     The Creation of Man and    Genesis 2:4–24
      Woman

4     Purpose of Creation        Reasons why God created human beings; Songs about creation



5     Midterm Examinations       –

6     Marriage                   Meaning of marriage (Gen. 2:22-25); Functions of marriage
                                 (Eph. 5:21-31)

7     Midterm Break              –

8     Marriage II                Types of marriage; Importance of marriage

9     Disobedience I             Meaning of disobedience; Adam & Eve’s disobedience (Gen.
                                 3:1-19)

10    Disobedience II            Consequences of Adam & Eve’s disobedience; Human
                                 disobedience and national disobedience

JSS 1 CHRISTIAN RELIGIOUS STUDIES (CRS) SCHEME OF WORK
SECOND TERM

Week Topic                     Content

1     Call to Repentance I     Meaning of repentance; King David’s repentance (2 Sam. 12:7–
                               13; Psalm 51)


2     Call to Repentance II    The people of Nineveh repent (Jonah 3:1-10)

3     Call to Repentance III   John the Baptist calls for repentance (Luke 3:1-14)

4     Call to Repentance IV    Jesus commends Zacchaeus (Luke 19:8); Consequences of lack of
                               repentance (1 Sam. 3:18; 4:10-21)


5     Midterm Examinations     –

6     Call to Obedience I      Meaning of obedience; Abraham obeys God’s call (Gen. 12:1-9)



7     Midterm Break            –

8     Call to Obedience II     Abraham’s blessings (Gen. 17:1-21); Abraham’s sacrifice of Isaac
                               (Gen. 21:1-8; 22:1-19)


9     Call to Service I        Meaning of service; Joseph’s and Ruth’s examples (Gen. 37:12-
                               36; 41:37-57; Ruth 1:15; 2:7-18)


10    Call to Service II       Lessons from Joseph & Ruth; Life in God’s service (1 Pet. 2:18-
                               25); Problems in service; Call to service


11–13 Revision, Examinations   –
      & Closing

JSS 1 ISLAMIC RELIGIOUS STUDIES (IRS) SCHEME OF WORK
FIRST TERM
Week Topic                                 Breakdown (Subtopics)
1     Introduction to the Qur’an           • Meaning and importance of the Qur’an• Revelation
                                           and preservation of the Qur’an• Difference between the
                                           Qur’an and Hadith

2     Qur’anic Recitation: Suratul Tariq   • Recitation and memorization• Translation and
      (Q86:1–17)                           interpretation• Lessons on Allah’s power and human
                                           accountability

3     Qur’anic Recitation: Suratul Buruj   • Recitation and memorization• Explanation of
      (Q85:1–22)                           meanings• Lessons on patience and steadfastness

4     Qur’anic Recitation: Suratul         • Recitation and memorization• Explanation of
      Inshiqaq (Q84:1–25)                  meanings• Lessons on judgment and the Hereafter

5     MID-TERM EXAMINATION                 Assessment of Qur’anic Recitation and Interpretation
                                           (Surahs 84–86)
6     Introduction to Tajwid               • Meaning and importance of Tajwid• Rules of
                                           pronunciation and fluency• Common errors in recitation

7     MID-TERM BREAK                       Holiday/Rest
8     Rules of Nun Sakinah and             • Idgham, Ikhfa, Izhar, Iqlab• Examples from the
      Tanwin                               Qur’an• Class recitation practice

9     Rules of Mim Sakinah                 • Idgham Shafawi, Ikhfa Shafawi, Izhar Shafawi•
                                           Practical exercises using selected verses

10    Importance of Tajwid                 • Benefits of correct recitation• Role of Tajwid in
                                           preserving the Qur’an
11    REVISION                             Comprehensive review of Surahs and Tajwid rules
12    EXAMINATION                          End of Term Assessment
13    CLOSING/VACATION                     Term ends

Week Topic                          Breakdown (Subtopics)

                                    Fiqh in Muslim life

      (Nawafil)

                                    significance

      Iman                          Importance in Muslim life

      EXAMINATION

                                    hypocrisy

9     Review of Qur’an and Hadith   • Integration of faith and practice• Recitation exercises

11    REVISION                      Comprehensive review of Fiqh, Iman, and ethics

JSS1 NIGERIAN HISTORY SCHEME OF WORK

JSS2 MATHEMATICS SCHEME OF WORK
FIRST TERM
Week Topic                    Content
1     Basic Operation of      Whole numbers in standard forms; decimal numbers in standard
      Integers / Whole        forms.
      Numbers I

      Integers / Whole
                              II.Continuation of operations on whole numbers with emphasis on
      Numbers II
                              addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division.

3     LCM & HCF               Lowest Common Multiple (LCM); Highest Common Factor
                              (HCF); introduction to square and square roots; application in
                              quantitative reasoning.

4     Fractions –             Expressing fractions as ratios, decimals, and percentages;
      Transactions in Homes   application of fractions in daily transactions; quantitative reasoning
      and Offices             on fractions, ratios, and percentages.

5     Midterm Examination     —
6     Approximation           Approximation of numbers to decimal places, significant figures,
                              whole numbers, nearest tens, hundreds, thousands, tenths,
                              hundredths, and thousandths; application in quantitative
                              reasoning.

7     Midterm Break           —
8     Directed and Non-       Definition and examples of directed and non-directed numbers; use
      Directed Numbers        of square and square root tables.

9     Multiplication and      Multiplication and division of directed and non-directed numbers
      Division of Directed    with practical exercises.
      Numbers

10    Algebraic Expressions   Definition of algebraic expressions with examples; expression of
      I                       algebraic forms; factorization of simple algebraic expressions.
11    Algebraic Expressions   Expansion and factorization of quadratic expressions; definition of
      II                      algebraic fractions; addition and subtraction of algebraic fractions
                              with whole number denominators; application of quantitative
                              reasoning in solving algebraic fraction problems.

12    Revision and            General revision of all topics taught during the term followed by
      Examination             end-of-term examination and closing activities.

13    Closing

JSS2 MATHEMATICS SCHEME OF WORK
SECOND TERM
Week Topic                    Content
1    Review of First Term’s   Emphasis on algebraic expressions, solving of quadratic
     Work                     equations, and transactions at home and offices.

2    Simple Equations         Meaning of algebraic equations; differences between algebraic
                              expressions and algebraic equations; solving problems on simple
                              algebraic equations.

3    Linear Inequalities I    Definition of linear inequalities; introduction to symbols and
                              basic properties of inequalities.

4    Linear Inequalities II   Word problems leading to simple inequalities in one variable;
                              solving and interpreting results.

5    Midterm Examination      —
6    Graphs I                 Graphical representation; graphs of Cartesian plane (the axis);
                              introduction to graphs of linear equations in two variables.

7    Midterm Break            —
8    Graphs II                Plotting of linear graphs in two variables from real-life situations;
                              solving quantitative reasoning problems on graphs.

9    Plane Figures/Shapes     Identifying plane shapes in the environment; properties of plane
                              shapes e.g. square, rectangle, parallelogram, rhombus, and kite.

10   Scale Drawing of         Meaning and purpose of scale drawing; measurement and
     Length and Distances     representation on paper.

11   Quantitative Aptitude    Meaning of quantitative aptitude; reasons for studying it; solving
                              problems related to plane shapes/figures and scale.
12   Revision and             General revision of all topics taught during the term and Second
     Examination              Term Examination

13   Closing                  Vacation activities.

SS1 MATHEMATICS SCHEME OF WORK
FIRST TERM
Theme: Number and Numeration / Algebraic Processes / Logic
Week Topic                    Content
1       Number Base           - Conversion from one base to base 10 - Conversion of decimal
        System                fractions in one base to another - Arithmetic operations in number
                              bases - Application to computer programming

2       Modular Arithmetic    - Revision of arithmetic operations - Concept of modular arithmetic -
                              Simple operations in modular arithmetic - Applications to daily life

3       Indices               - Revision of standard form - Laws of indices with examples -
                              Applications of indices in problem solving

4       Logarithms            - Definition of logarithm - Relationship between logarithm, indices
                              and standard form - Graph of 10^x - Reading logarithm and
                              antilogarithm tables

5       Midterm               —
        Examination

6       Effective Use of      - Use of logarithm tables in calculations (division, multiplication,
        Logarithm Tables      powers, roots) - Applications of logarithms in capital markets and
                              real-life problem solving

7       Midterm Break         —
8       Simple Equations      - Change of subject of formula (involving roots, brackets, powers) -
        and Variations        Substitution - Direct, inverse and joint variation - Applications of
                              variation

9       Quadratic             - Revision of factorization of integers - Factorization of quadratic
        Equations I           expressions - Solution of quadratic equations by factorization

10      Quadratic             - Formation of quadratic equations with given roots - Drawing
        Equations II          quadratic graphs - Obtaining roots from graphs - Applications of

       Not Die II                         issues in the text

SS3 BIOLOGY SCHEME OF WORK
FIRST TERM
WEEK TOPIC                       CONTENT BREAKDOWN
1    Reproductive                Meaning of reproductive behaviour; courtship behaviour;
     Behaviours in Animals       mating systems (monogamy, polygamy, promiscuity); parental
                                 care in fishes, amphibians, birds and mammals; importance of
                                 reproductive behaviours in survival of species.
2      Biology of Heredity       Concept of heredity; brief history of genetics (Gregor Mendel’s
       (Genetics I)              experiments); terminologies—gene, allele, chromosome,
                                 genotype, phenotype, homozygous, heterozygous; Mendel’s
                                 laws of inheritance with examples.
3      Genetics II               Monohybrid and dihybrid crosses; Punnett square applications;
                                 sex determination in humans; sex-linked traits (haemophilia,
                                 colour blindness); importance of genetics in agriculture and
                                 medicine.
4      Variation and Evolution   Concept of variation; types of variation—morphological,
       I                         physiological, discontinuous and continuous; causes of
                                 variation (mutation, meiosis, fertilization, environment);
                                 adaptive colouration, mimicry, and camouflage in animals.
5      Midterm Examination       —
6      Evolution II              Meaning of evolution; theories of evolution—Lamarckism and
                                 Darwinism; modern synthetic theory of evolution; evidence of
                                 evolution (fossil records, comparative anatomy, embryology,
                                 vestigial structures); natural selection and adaptation.
7      Midterm Break             —
8      Human Evolution           Origin of man; fossil evidence of human evolution
                                 (Australopithecus, Homo habilis, Homo erectus, Homo
                                 sapiens); similarities between humans and apes; cultural and
                                 biological evolution of humans.
9      Application of Genetics   Application in agriculture (selective breeding, hybridization,
       and Evolution             genetic engineering, GMOs); application in medicine (blood
                                 transfusion, sickle cell study, genetic counselling, cloning, IVF);
                                 role of evolution in biodiversity and conservation.
10     Ethical, Social and       Ethical debates on cloning, stem cell research, GMOs;
       Economic Implications     advantages and disadvantages; importance of conservation of
       of Genetics and           genetic resources; relevance to Nigeria’s development.
       Evolution
11     Revision                  General revision of all topics taught in the term, with practice
                                 questions and past exam applications.
12     Examination               —
13     Closing                   —

SS3 LITERATURE-IN-ENGLISH SCHEME OF WORK
FIRST TERM
Week Topic                                   Content Breakdown
1    Welcome Test / Revision of Last         - Recall the previous term’s work.
     Term’s Work                             -Relate Shakespeare’s background to Antony and
                                             Cleopatra.
                                             -Discuss the play’s historical and thematic
                                             background.
2      Dramatic Personae of William          -Analyse the characters: Antony, Cleopatra, Octavius
       Shakespeare’s Antony and Cleopatra    Caesar, Enobarbus, Charmian, and others.
                                             -Discuss relationships among the key characters and
                                             their roles in the tragedy.
3      Antony and Cleopatra – Act I          - Analyse the events and conflicts in Act I.
                                             -Discuss Antony’s divided loyalty between Rome and
                                             Egypt.
                                             - Examine themes of love, power, and politics.
4      Antony and Cleopatra – Act II         -Discuss the growing tension between Antony and
                                             Caesar.
                                             -Analyse Cleopatra’s character and her influence on
                                             Antony.
                                             -Examine language, imagery, and dramatic
                                             techniques used.
5      Antony and Cleopatra – Act III        - Study the battle scenes and turning points.
                                             - Examine betrayal, ambition, and loyalty.
                                             - Discuss the role of Enobarbus as a foil to Antony.
6      Antony and Cleopatra – Acts IV & V    - Analyse the tragic resolution of the play.
                                             -Identify the main themes: love vs. duty, honour,
                                             pride, and fate.
                                             - Discuss Shakespeare’s use of imagery, irony, and
                                             symbolism.
7      Mid-Term Break / Open Day             —
8      Introduction to Unseen Prose          - Features of unseen prose: comprehension and plot.
                                             -Tools for unseen passage analysis: theme, setting,
                                             tone, and character.
                                             - Practice analysis using short unseen prose excerpts.
9–10   Introduction to Unseen Poem           -Features of unseen poem: structure, imagery, and
                                             mood.
                                             - Literary devices: metaphor, personification, simile,
                                             irony, alliteration, paradox.
                                             -Practice unseen poem analysis using short modern
                                             poems.
11     Principles of Answering Context and   - Interpret common exam verbs (comment, describe,
       Literature Questions                  contrast, examine).

WEEK 1: Introduction to Chemistry
Definition, History, Branches, and Importance


DEFINITION OF CHEMISTRY
Chemistry is the branch of science that deals with the study of the composition, structure, properties, and
changes (reactions) of matter.

It seeks to understand:
      What matter is made of (composition)
      How matter is arranged (structure)
      How matter behaves (properties)
      How matter changes (chemical reactions)
Chemistry is often called the “central science” because it connects and overlaps with other sciences like
Physics, Biology, Medicine, and Geology.




HISTORY OF CHEMISTRY
The history of chemistry can be divided into several stages:
a. Ancient Period (Before 16th Century)
      Early humans used natural substances such as fire, salt, and metals like gold and copper without
       understanding their composition.
      The practice of alchemy emerged, combining elements of magic, religion, and early
       experimentation.

      Alchemists sought to transform base metals (like lead) into gold and to discover the elixir of life.

                                                     453
             GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




b. Transition Period (16th–18th Century)
      The idea of experimentation and observation started replacing mystical beliefs.
      Robert Boyle (1627–1691) is regarded as the father of modern chemistry for his emphasis on
       experimentation and his famous Boyle’s Law on gases.
      Antoine Lavoisier (1743–1794), called the father of modern chemistry, named “oxygen” and
       “hydrogen,” and established the Law of Conservation of Mass.

c. Modern Period (19th Century – Present)
      Development of the Atomic Theory by John Dalton (1808).
      Discovery of the Periodic Table by Dmitri Mendeleev (1869).
      Advancements in physical chemistry, organic chemistry, and biochemistry.
      In the 20th century, chemistry expanded into nuclear chemistry, polymer chemistry, and
       analytical chemistry.




BRANCHES OF CHEMISTRY
Chemistry is broadly divided into the following branches:

                                                   454
              GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




a. Physical Chemistry
Deals with the physical properties and behavior of matter, energy changes, and reaction rates.
Examples: study of thermodynamics, chemical equilibrium, and kinetics.

b. Organic Chemistry
Study of compounds containing carbon (except carbonates and oxides of carbon).
Examples: hydrocarbons, alcohols, and plastics.

c. Inorganic Chemistry
Study of elements and compounds that are not based on carbon.
Examples: metals, salts, and minerals.

d. Analytical Chemistry
Focuses on methods used to determine the composition of substances (qualitative and quantitative
analysis).
Examples: titration, spectroscopy, chromatography.
e. Biochemistry
Study of chemical processes in living organisms.
Examples: digestion, respiration, and photosynthesis.

f. Industrial Chemistry
Application of chemical principles in industries to produce useful materials such as fertilizers, paints,
drugs, and soaps.



IMPORTANCE OF CHEMISTRY
Chemistry plays a vital role in everyday life and national development.
a. In Medicine and Health
      Production of drugs, vaccines, and anesthetics.
      Development of disinfectants and diagnostic chemicals.
b. In Agriculture
      Manufacture of fertilizers, pesticides, and herbicides.
      Development of soil analysis and irrigation chemicals.
c. In Industry
      Production of plastics, paints, soap, cement, textiles, and cosmetics.


                                                      455
             GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




      Extraction and purification of metals.
d. In Environment
      Helps to understand and control pollution.
      Development of renewable energy sources.
e. In Food and Nutrition
      Preservation and processing of food.
      Production of flavorings and supplements.
f. In Scientific Research
      Provides a foundation for innovations in energy, materials, and biotechnology.




EVALUATION
   1. Define chemistry and explain why it is called the “central science.”
   2. Outline the three main historical stages of chemistry and name one scientist associated with each
      stage.

   3. Differentiate between organic and inorganic chemistry with examples.
   4. Mention any four branches of chemistry and state one importance of each.
   5. List five areas of human life where chemistry plays a major role.


ASSIGNMENT
   1. Write short notes on the contributions of Robert Boyle and Antoine Lavoisier to modern
      chemistry.

   2. Make a neat diagram showing the major branches of chemistry.
   3. Identify five industrial products made possible through chemistry and state their uses.

             GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




WEEK 2 – CHEMICAL INDUSTRIES AND LABORATORY APPARATUS

CHEMICAL INDUSTRIES

Meaning of Chemical Industries
Chemical industries are industries that use chemical substances at one stage or another to produce other
chemicals or finished goods.

      Raw materials: Substances used as starting materials in production.
      Products: Finished goods from an industry, which may also serve as raw materials for another
       industry.

Types of Raw Materials

   1. Natural raw materials – Obtained directly from nature without major alteration.
      Examples: air, water, coal, limestone, petroleum, iron ore, sulphur deposits.
   2. Synthetic raw materials – Man-made materials produced for further manufacturing.
      Examples: synthetic fibres, plastics, palm kernel oil for soaps, thread for textiles.

Classification of Chemical Industries

   1. Heavy Chemicals – Needed in large quantities; purity is not the top priority.
      Examples: tetraoxosulphate (VI) acid (H₂SO₄), sodium hydroxide, cement, palm kernel oil.
   2. Fine Chemicals – Required in small quantities; produced in very pure form.
      Examples: sodium chloride (NaCl), dyes, preservatives, analytical reagents.

Divisions According to Products

      Glass industries – Use sand, clay, sodium carbonate, and colourants to make glass.
      Ceramics industries – Use clay and fine chemicals to make ceramics.
      Pharmaceutical industries – Use organic, inorganic, and synthetic chemicals to produce drugs.
      Metallurgical industries – Extract and process metals from ores.
      Fertilizer industries – Produce nitrogen, phosphorus, potassium compounds for agriculture.
      Paint industries – Use pigments and inorganic chemicals to make paints.

Importance of Chemical Industries

      Provide raw materials for other industries.
      Generate income and employment.
      Serve as research centres and educational resources.

Disadvantages of Chemical Industries

      Cause environmental pollution.
      Can lead to rural-urban migration.
      Capital intensive to establish.
                                                    457
              GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE

Laboratory work in chemistry requires various apparatus for measuring, holding, heating, mixing, and

Practical Safety Tips for Students:

      Always wear safety goggles and a lab coat.
      Do not heat a closed container.
      Handle acids and alkalis with care.
      Always read labels on reagent bottles before use.

             GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




      Never taste or directly smell chemicals; waft gently if necessary.

WEEK 3 - NATURE OF MATTER – STATES, PROPERTIES, AND CHANGES OF STATE

Meaning of Matter

Matter is anything that has mass and occupies space. All physical objects—whether visible (like a table) or
invisible (like air)—are made of matter. Matter is composed of tiny particles called atoms and molecules
that are in constant motion.



States of Matter

Matter exists in three primary states: solid, liquid, and gas. In advanced science, two more states—plasma
and Bose–Einstein condensate—are recognized, but at the secondary school level, we focus on the main
three.

(a) Solid

      Particle arrangement: Particles are closely packed in a fixed, orderly arrangement.
      Movement: Particles vibrate in fixed positions.
      Shape and volume: Has a definite shape and definite volume.
      Compressibility: Cannot be easily compressed due to little space between particles.
      Examples: Wood, metal, stone, ice.

(b) Liquid

      Particle arrangement: Particles are close together but not in a fixed arrangement.
      Movement: Particles can slide over each other, allowing liquids to flow.
      Shape and volume: Has definite volume but no definite shape—it takes the shape of its container.
      Compressibility: Almost incompressible.
      Examples: Water, oil, alcohol, mercury.

(c) Gas

      Particle arrangement: Particles are far apart and not in a fixed position.
      Movement: Move freely at high speed in all directions.
      Shape and volume: Has neither definite shape nor definite volume—it expands to fill its container.
      Compressibility: Highly compressible due to large spaces between particles.
      Examples: Air, oxygen, carbon dioxide, nitrogen.

Properties of the States of Matter

            Property                 Solid                  Liquid                        Gas
 Shape                       Definite                Indefinite              Indefinite
 Volume                      Definite                Definite                Indefinite

                                                    461
              GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




 Arrangement of particles       Fixed, closely packed   Close, random           Far apart, random
 Movement of particles          Vibrate in place        Slide over each other   Move freely in all directions
 Density                        High                    Medium                  Low
 Compressibility                Very low                Very low                High
 Energy of particles            Lowest                  Moderate                Highest




Changes of State

Changes of state occur when matter is transformed from one state to another by heating or cooling. These
processes involve energy changes but do not alter the chemical composition of the substance.

(a) Melting

      Definition: Change from solid to liquid upon heating.
      Example: Ice melting into water.
      Energy change: Heat energy is absorbed to overcome the forces holding particles in fixed positions.

(b) Freezing (Solidification)

      Definition: Change from liquid to solid upon cooling.
      Example: Water freezing into ice.
      Energy change: Heat energy is released as particles slow down and become fixed.

(c) Vaporization (Boiling/Evaporation)

      Boiling: Rapid change from liquid to gas at a specific temperature (boiling point).
      Evaporation: Slow change from liquid to gas at the surface, at any temperature.

                                                        462
              GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




       Example: Water boiling at 100°C; drying of wet clothes.

(d) Condensation

       Definition: Change from gas to liquid upon cooling.
       Example: Water vapour forming droplets on a cold surface.

(e) Sublimation

       Definition: Direct change from solid to gas without passing through the liquid state.
       Examples: Iodine crystals, dry ice (solid CO₂), naphthalene balls.

(f) Deposition

       Definition: Change from gas directly to solid without becoming liquid.
       Example: Formation of frost.



Particle Theory of Matter

The Kinetic Theory of Matter explains that:

   1.   All matter is made up of tiny particles (atoms or molecules).
   2.   Particles are in constant motion.
   3.   The higher the temperature, the faster the particles move.
   4.   The state of matter depends on the spacing and movement of the particles.

Arrangement of particles in solids, liquids, and gases




Common Misconceptions

       Misconception: Gases have no mass.
        Correction: Gases have mass, though they are light and invisible.
       Misconception: Melting and dissolving are the same.
        Correction: Melting changes state due to heat; dissolving is mixing with a solvent.

                                                     463
              GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




Practical Examples

       Melting: Ice in a glass on a hot day.
       Boiling: Water boiling for cooking.
       Evaporation: Sweat drying from the skin.
       Condensation: Fogging of mirrors after a hot bath.
       Sublimation: Disappearance of mothballs in clothes.



Evaluation

   1. Define matter and list its three main states.
   2. Differentiate between solids, liquids, and gases using at least four properties.
   3. Explain the difference between boiling and evaporation.
   4. Give two examples each of:
          o Melting
          o Sublimation
          o Condensation
   5. State the particle arrangement and motion in gases.



Assignment

   1. With the aid of a diagram, show the particle arrangement in solids, liquids, and gases.
   2. State five differences between evaporation and boiling.
   3. Write short notes on the following changes of state: freezing, deposition, melting.

WEEK 4 – SEPARATION TECHNIQUES

In chemistry, separation techniques are methods used to divide mixtures into their individual components,
based on differences in physical or chemical properties such as particle size, density, solubility, boiling
point, or magnetic properties. The choice of a suitable technique depends on:

      The nature of the mixture (solid–solid, solid–liquid, liquid–liquid, gas mixtures).
      The properties of the components.
      The level of purity required.




1. Filtration

      Definition: A process of separating an insoluble solid from a liquid using a porous barrier (filter
       paper).
      Principle: Based on particle size — solid particles are too large to pass through the filter pores.
      Example: Separating sand from water.




2. Distillation

                                                     465
               GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




        Definition: Separation of a liquid from a mixture using differences in boiling points.
        Principle: Components with lower boiling points vaporize first, then condense back to liquid.
        Example: Purifying water from saltwater.




3.                                                       Fractional Distillation

        Definition: A refined form of distillation used to separate a mixture of liquids with close boiling
         points.
        Principle: Utilizes a fractionating column to allow repeated condensation and vaporization for
         better separation.
        Example: Separating ethanol from water, crude oil refining.




4.                                                       Sublimation

        Definition: Direct conversion of a solid to gas without passing through the liquid phase.

                                                       466
               GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




        Principle: Based on differences in sublimation tendencies between substances.
        Example: Separating iodine crystals from a mixture.




5. Chromatography

        Definition: Technique for separating components of a mixture based on their movement through a
         medium.
        Principle: Components move at different rates due to differences in solubility and adsorption.
        Example: Separating plant pigments on paper.




6.                                                Centrifugation

                                                          Definition: Separation of mixtures using
         centrifugal force by spinning at high speed.
        Principle: Denser particles move outward faster than less dense ones.
        Example: Separating plasma from blood cells.



                                                     467
             GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




7. Decantation

      Definition: Pouring off a liquid to separate it from sediment or an immiscible liquid.
      Principle: Relies on density differences between substances.
      Example: Removing water from settled sand.




8. Evaporation

      Definition: Removal of a liquid by heating until only the solid residue remains.
      Principle: Components with lower boiling points evaporate, leaving non-volatile solids behind.
      Example: Obtaining salt from seawater.




                                                    468
             GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




9. Magnetic Separation

      Definition: Use of magnets to separate magnetic materials from non-magnetic ones.
      Principle: Based on magnetic properties of substances.
      Example: Separating iron filings from sand.




                                                                                10. Winnowing

      Definition: Winnowing is a separation technique used to separate lighter particles (such as husks
       or chaff) from heavier particles (such as grains or seeds) using the action of wind or air currents.
      Principle: The method works on the difference in weight (density) between the two substances —
       lighter materials are blown away by air, while heavier materials fall straight down.
      Example: Separating paddy rice grains from husk after threshing.




                                                    469
              GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




11. Sieving

      Definition: Sieving is a separation technique used to separate particles of different sizes by
       passing a mixture through a sieve or mesh that allows smaller particles to pass through while
       retaining the larger ones.
      Principle: The method works based on the difference in particle size.
      Example: Separating wheat flour from bran using a fine mesh sieve.




12. Sedimentation

      Definition: Sedimentation is a separation technique in which insoluble, heavier particles in a
       liquid settle at the bottom under the influence of gravity.
      Principle: The method is based on the difference in density between solid particles and the liquid.




                                                    470
              GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




       Example: Allowing muddy water to stand so that sand and mud settle at the bottom.




Evaluation

   1.   Define separation techniques and state three factors that determine the choice of method.
   2.   Distinguish between simple distillation and fractional distillation.
   3.   Give two examples of substances that can be separated by sublimation.
   4.   Explain the principle behind chromatography.
   5.   Name three separation techniques based on density differences.



Assignment

       Draw and label the apparatus for filtration, simple distillation, chromatography, and separating
        funnel method.
       Research two industrial applications of fractional distillation and write short notes on them.

              GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




WEEK 6 – ATOMIC STRUCTURE – STRUCTURE OF THE ATOM, SUBATOMIC PARTICLES


Meaning of Atom
      An atom is the smallest particle of an element that takes part in chemical reactions and retains the
       chemical properties of that element.

      Atoms are extremely small: e.g., radius ≈ 10⁻¹⁰ m.




Historical Development of the Atomic Model
   1. Dalton’s Atomic Theory (1803)
          o   Atoms are indivisible, indestructible particles.
          o   Atoms of the same element are identical; atoms of different elements differ.
          o   Atoms combine in simple ratios to form compounds.
          o   Limitation: Failed to explain subatomic particles and isotopes.
   2. Thomson’s Model (1897) – Plum pudding model
          o   Discovered the electron.
          o   Proposed that an atom is a sphere of positive charge with electrons embedded.
          o   Limitation: Could not explain the stability of atoms.
   3. Rutherford’s Model (1911) – Nuclear model


                                                     472
                GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




            o    Gold foil experiment showed atoms are mostly empty space.
            o    Nucleus at the center, containing positive charges (protons).
            o    Electrons revolve around nucleus like planets.
            o    Limitation: Could not explain why electrons don’t collapse into the nucleus.
   4. Bohr’s Model (1913) – Planetary model
            o    Electrons revolve in fixed orbits (energy levels/shells).
            o    Energy is absorbed or released when electrons jump between shells.
            o    Limitation: Could not fully explain multi-electron atoms.
   5. Modern Atomic Theory (Quantum Mechanical Model)
            o    Developed by Schrödinger, Heisenberg, etc.
            o    Electrons are in regions called orbitals, not fixed paths.
            o    Atoms consist of a dense nucleus (protons + neutrons) with electrons in shells/subshells.


Subatomic Particles
 Particle   Symbol Charge Relative Mass                                 Location in Atom
 Proton     p⁺          +1         1 a.m.u. (≈1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ kg)          In nucleus
 Neutron n⁰             0          1 a.m.u. (≈1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ kg)          In nucleus
 Electron e⁻            –1         1/1836 a.m.u. (≈9.11 × 10⁻³¹ kg)     Orbiting nucleus in shells
      Protons: Determine the identity of an element (Atomic number).
      Neutrons: Provide stability in the nucleus.
      Electrons: Involved in chemical bonding and reactions.


Atomic Number and Mass Number
      Atomic Number (Z): Number of protons in the nucleus.
      Mass Number (A): Number of protons + neutrons.
      Neutron number (N): N = A – Z.
Example: Carbon-12 has Z = 6, A = 12 → Protons = 6, Neutrons = 6, Electrons = 6.



                                                        473
               GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




Isotopes
      Atoms of the same element (same Z) but different mass numbers (A).
      Example: ¹²C, ¹³C, ¹⁴C are isotopes of carbon.
      They have similar chemical properties but different physical properties.


Atomic Structure Representation
      Electrons are arranged in shells (K, L, M, N) according to the 2n² rule:
           o   K shell → 2 electrons
           o   L shell → 8 electrons
           o   M shell → 18 electrons
           o   N shell → 32 electrons
Example:
      Sodium (Z = 11) → 2, 8, 1
      Oxygen (Z = 8) → 2, 6




EVALUATION
   1. Define an atom.
   2. State three limitations of Dalton’s Atomic Theory.
   3. Mention the scientist who discovered the electron.
   4. Distinguish between atomic number and mass number.
   5. Write the electronic configuration of:
           o   Magnesium (Z = 12)

                                                    474
                  GET ACCESS TO MORE EDUCATIONAL RESOURCES - CLICK HERE




          o       Chlorine (Z = 17)
  6. Fill in the table:
Element Atomic Number Mass Number Protons Neutrons Electrons
Sodium        11                  23           ?          ?           ?
Oxygen        8                   16           ?          ?           ?

  2. Write the electronic configurations of the following elements:
